The equation of the parabola with vertex at (0, 0) and focus at (0, 4) is determined by the formula \( x^2 = 16y \).

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the Direction:** The focus (0,4) lies above the vertex (0,0), so the parabola opens upward.

2. **Use Standard Form:** For a parabola opening upwards with vertex at (h,k), the standard form is \( (x - h)^2 = 4p(y - k) \).

3. **Plug in Vertex Coordinates:** Substitute (h,k) as (0,0): \( x^2 = 4py \).

4. **Determine p:** The distance from vertex to focus is p. Since the focus is at (0,4), p=4.

5. **Substitute p into Equation:** Replace p with 4: \( x^2 = 16y \).

**Final Answer:**  
The equation of the parabola is \( x^2 = 16y \).

First off, I remember that a parabola is defined as the set of all points where the distance from each point on the parabola to the focus is equal to the distance from that point to the directrix. So, for any point (x,y) on the parabola, it should satisfy this condition.

The focus is given as (2,3), and the directrix is a vertical line x = -1. Since the directrix is vertical, the parabola must open either to the right or to the left. That makes sense because if the directrix were horizontal, the parabola would open up or down.

Now, I need to set up the equation using the distance formulas. The distance from any point (x,y) to the focus (2,3) can be calculated using the distance formula: sqrt[(x-2)^2 + (y-3)^2]. 

The distance from the point (x,y) to the directrix x = -1 is a bit different since it's a vertical line. The formula for this horizontal distance is simply the absolute value of the difference in the x-coordinates, so that would be |x - (-1)| which simplifies to |x + 1|.

Since these two distances must be equal for all points on the parabola, I can set them equal to each other. So:

sqrt[(x-2)^2 + (y-3)^2] = |x + 1|

To eliminate the square root and absolute value, I'll square both sides of the equation. Squaring the left side gives me [(x-2)^2 + (y-3)^2], and squaring the right side would give (x+1)^2 because (|a|)^2 = a^2.

So now my equation is:

(x - 2)^2 + (y - 3)^2 = (x + 1)^2

Let me expand both sides to simplify this equation. Starting with the left side:

(x - 2)^2 = x^2 - 4x + 4
(y - 3)^2 = y^2 - 6y + 9

Adding these together, I get:
x^2 - 4x + 4 + y^2 - 6y + 9 = x^2 - 4x + y^2 - 6y + 13

On the right side:

(x + 1)^2 = x^2 + 2x + 1

So now, putting it all together:

x^2 - 4x + y^2 - 6y + 13 = x^2 + 2x + 1

I can subtract x^2 from both sides to simplify further:
-4x + y^2 - 6y + 13 = 2x + 1

Now, let's move all terms to the left side:
-4x - 2x + y^2 - 6y + 13 - 1 = 0
Which simplifies to:
-6x + y^2 - 6y + 12 = 0

y^2 - 6y - 6x + 12 = 0

I think completing the square for the y terms would help here. So let's focus on the y part: y^2 - 6y.

To complete the square, I take half of the coefficient of y, which is -3, and square it to get 9. So I'll add and subtract 9:

(y^2 - 6y + 9) - 9

This becomes (y - 3)^2 - 9.

Now substituting back into the equation:

(y - 3)^2 - 9 - 6x + 12 = 0
Simplifying the constants: -9 + 12 is 3.
So now it's:
(y - 3)^2 - 6x + 3 = 0

Let me rearrange this to solve for x:

-6x = -(y - 3)^2 - 3

Multiply both sides by -1 to make it positive:

6x = (y - 3)^2 + 3

Now, divide both sides by 6 to isolate x:

x = [(y - 3)^2 + 3] / 6

This can be written as:
x = [(y - 3)^2]/6 + 3/6
Simplifying further:
x = (y - 3)^2/(6) + 1/2

Hmm, but usually parabolas are expressed in terms of y^2 or x^2. Maybe I can write it as:

(y - 3)^2 = 6(x - 1/2)

That looks more familiar because it's similar to the standard form (y - k)^2 = 4p(x - h), where (h, k) is the vertex and p is the distance from the vertex to the focus.

Comparing, my equation is (y - 3)^2 = 6(x - 1/2). So here, 4p = 6, which means p = 6/4 = 3/2. The vertex should be halfway between the focus and the directrix.

The focus is at (2,3), so moving towards the directrix x=-1, the vertex would be located at a point that's midway in the x-direction but same y-coordinate. Let me calculate it:

Midpoint between x = 2 (focus) and x = -1 (directrix). The distance between them is 3 units along the x-axis. So halfway would be 1.5 units from each.

So starting at x=2, moving 1.5 units towards x=-1 gives x=0.5 or 1/2. Therefore, the vertex is at (1/2, 3).

That makes sense because in my equation, it's (y - 3)^2 = 6(x - 1/2), so h = 1/2 and k = 3.

Vertex at (1/2,3), adding p=3/2 along x gives (1/2 + 3/2) = 2. That's correct for the focus.

The directrix is located p units to the left of the vertex, so that would be x = h - p = 1/2 - 3/2 = -1. Perfect, matches the given directrix.

So everything checks out.

To find the vertex (शीर्ष) form of the given quadratic equation \( Y = 3x^2 + 6x + 5 \), we can complete the square.

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Start with the given equation:**
   \[
   Y = 3x^2 + 6x + 5
   \]

2. **Factor out the coefficient of \( x^2 \) from the first two terms:**
   \[
   Y = 3(x^2 + 2x) + 5
   \]

3. **Complete the square inside the parentheses:**
   - Take half of the coefficient of \( x \), which is \( 1 \).
   - Square it to get \( 1 \).

4. **Add and subtract this square inside the parentheses:**
   \[
   Y = 3\left( (x^2 + 2x + 1) - 1 \right) + 5
   \]
   \[
   Y = 3(x + 1)^2 - 3 + 5
   \]

5. **Combine like terms:**
   \[
   Y = 3(x + 1)^2 + 2
   \]

**Final Answer:**

\boxed{Y = 3(x + 1)^2 + 2}

To convert the given quadratic equation into **vertex form**, follow these steps:

**Given Equation:**
\[ x^2 - 4x + 8y + 12 = 0 \]

**Step 1:** Rearrange the terms to isolate \( y \).
\[
x^2 - 4x + 12 = -8y
\]
\[
-8y = x^2 - 4x + 12
\]

**Step 2:** Divide each term by \(-8\) to solve for \( y \).
\[
y = \frac{x^2}{-8} + \frac{4x}{-8} - \frac{12}{-8}
\]
Simplify:
\[
y = -\frac{1}{8}x^2 + \frac{1}{2}x + \frac{3}{2}
\]

**Step 3:** Identify the coefficients.
The equation is now in standard form \( y = ax^2 + bx + c \), where:
- \( a = -\frac{1}{8} \)
- \( b = \frac{1}{2} \)
- \( c = \frac{3}{2} \)

**Step 4:** Complete the square to convert into vertex form.

Start with:
\[
y = -\frac{1}{8}x^2 + \frac{1}{2}x + \frac{3}{2}
\]

Factor out \( a \) from the first two terms:
\[
y = -\frac{1}{8}\left(x^2 - 4x\right) + \frac{3}{2}
\]

Complete the square inside the parentheses:
- Take half of the coefficient of \( x \): \( \frac{-4}{2} = -2 \)
- Square it: \( (-2)^2 = 4 \)

Add and subtract this value inside the parentheses:
\[
y = -\frac{1}{8}\left(x^2 - 4x + 4 - 4\right) + \frac{3}{2}
\]
Simplify:
\[
y = -\frac{1}{8}\left((x - 2)^2 - 4\right) + \frac{3}{2}
\]

Distribute \( -\frac{1}{8} \):
\[
y = -\frac{1}{8}(x - 2)^2 + \frac{1}{2} + \frac{3}{2}
\]
Combine constants:
\[
y = -\frac{1}{8}(x - 2)^2 + 2
\]

**Final Vertex Form:**
\[
y = -\frac{1}{8}(x - 2)^2 + 2
\]

The equation is now in **vertex form**, where the vertex of the parabola is at \( (h, k) = (2, 2) \).

**Answer:**  
\boxed{\displaystyle y = -\frac{1}{8}(x - 2)^2 + 2}

Alternatively, maybe this problem setup is invalid because for a non-degenerate parabola, focus cannot lie on directrix. Because if the focus lies on directrix, then all points equidistant would be along x=0 as shown earlier, making a straight line rather than a curve.

Therefore, perhaps such a parabola doesn't exist or is degenerate into a straight line.
</think>

The given problem asks for the equation of a parabola with focus at (0, 3) and directrix y = 3. However, upon setting up the equations based on the definition of a parabola as the set of all points equidistant from the focus and directrix, we find that the only solutions are points along the line x=0.

Step-by-Step Explanation:

1. **Definition**: A parabola is defined as the set of all points where each point's distance to the focus equals its perpendicular distance to the directrix.

2. **Given**:
   - Focus at (h, k) = (0, 3)
   - Directrix: y = 3

3. **Equation Setup**:
   - For any point P(x, y), compute distances.
     - Distance from P to focus: sqrt[(x - 0)^2 + (y - 3)^2]
     - Distance from P to directrix: |y - 3|
   
4. **Setting Distances Equal**:
   ```
   sqrt(x² + (y - 3)²) = |y - 3|
   ```

5. **Squaring Both Sides**:
   ```
   x² + (y - 3)² = (y - 3)²
   => x² = 0
   => x = 0
   ```

6. **Conclusion**: The equation simplifies to x=0, which is a vertical line along the y-axis.

**Final Answer**
The equation of the parabola under these conditions is simply the line x=0. However, in standard conic section definitions, this represents a degenerate case where the focus lies on the directrix, resulting in no curvature and effectively being a straight line instead of a typical parabolic curve.

$\boxed{x = 0}$

Which simplifies to:
4a + 2b + c = 8

For the point (-1, 3):
a(-1)² + b(-1) + c = 3
Which simplifies to:
a - b + c = 3

For the point (0, 4):
a(0)² + b(0) + c = 4
Which simplifies directly to:
c = 4

4a + 2b + 4 = 8
Subtracting 4 from both sides gives:
4a + 2b = 4
Dividing by 2 simplifies to:
2a + b = 2

From the second equation:
a - b + 4 = 3
Subtracting 4 from both sides gives:
a - b = -1

Now I have a system of two equations with two variables:

2a + b = 2  
a - b = -1

I'll add these two equations to eliminate b:

3a = 1

Substituting a = 1/3 back into the second equation:
(1/3) - b = -1  
Which leads to:  
b = 4/3

Now that I've found a, b, and c:

a = 1/3  
b = 4/3  
c = 4

The final equation of the parabola is y = (1/3)x² + (4/3)x + 4.

\( \text{Vertex's y-coordinate} = \frac{-1 + (-4)}{2} = -2.5 \)

   \[

Next, I solve for p by equating \( 4p = -16 \), which gives me \( p = -4 \). The value of p represents the distance from the vertex to the focus and also determines the direction in which the parabola opens. Since p is negative, the focus is located below the vertex.

To determine the **vertex** and **focus** of the parabola defined by the equation \( X^2 = -16Y \), we'll follow these steps:

### Step 1: Understand the Standard Form of a Parabola
The given equation is:
\[ X^2 = -16Y \]

This is in the standard form of a vertically oriented parabola:
\[ (X - h)^2 = 4p(Y - k) \]
where:
- \( (h, k) \) is the vertex.
- \( p \) determines the direction and distance from the vertex to the focus.

### Step 2: Identify the Vertex
Comparing the given equation with the standard form:
\[ X^2 = -16Y \]
We can see that it matches when \( h = 0 \), \( k = 0 \). Therefore, the vertex is at the origin.
\[ (h, k) = (0, 0) \]

### Step 3: Determine the Value of \( p \)
From the standard form:
\[ X^2 = 4pY \]
Comparing with our equation:
\[ -16Y = 4pY \]
Divide both sides by Y and simplify:
\[ 4p = -16 \]
Solve for \( p \):
\[ p = -4 \]

### Step 4: Find the Focus
Since the parabola opens downward (because \( p < 0 \)), the focus is located below the vertex at a distance of \( |p| \) units.

The coordinates of the focus are:
\[ (h, k + p) = (0, 0 - 4) = (0, -4) \]

### Final Answer
- **Vertex**: \((0, 0)\)
- **Focus**: \((0, -4)\)

To determine the center and radius of the circle given by the equation \( X^2 + Y^2 + 6X - 4Y - 12 = 0 \), we'll follow these steps:

### Step 1: Rewrite the Equation in Standard Form
The standard form of a circle's equation is:
\[ (x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2 \]
where \( (h, k) \) is the center and \( r \) is the radius.

Given equation:
\[ X^2 + Y^2 + 6X - 4Y - 12 = 0 \]

### Step 2: Complete the Square for \( X \) Terms
We'll group the \( X \) terms together:
\[ (X^2 + 6X) + Y^2 - 4Y - 12 = 0 \]

Complete the square by adding and subtracting \( (6/2)^2 = 9 \):
\[
(X^2 + 6X + 9 - 9) + Y^2 - 4Y - 12 = 0 \\
(X + 3)^2 - 9 + Y^2 - 4Y - 12 = 0
\]

### Step 3: Complete the Square for \( Y \) Terms
Now, group the \( Y \) terms:
\[ (X + 3)^2 + (Y^2 - 4Y) - 9 - 12 = 0 \]

Complete the square by adding and subtracting \( (-4/2)^2 = 4 \):
\[
(X + 3)^2 + (Y^2 - 4Y + 4 - 4) - 9 - 12 = 0 \\
(X + 3)^2 + (Y - 2)^2 - 16 = 0
\]

### Step 4: Rearrange to Standard Form
Move the constant term to the right side:
\[ (X + 3)^2 + (Y - 2)^2 = 16 \]

This is now in standard form, where:
- Center \( (h, k) = (-3, 2) \)
- Radius \( r = \sqrt{16} = 4 \)

### Final Answer
The circle has a center at \( (-3, 2) \) and a radius of 4.

\[
(x + 2)^2 + (y - 5)^2 = 25

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**  

1. **Identify the Standard Equation of a Circle**: The general equation for a circle with center \((h, k)\) and radius \(r\) is  
   \[
   (x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
   \]

2. **Substitute the Given Center**: Plugging in the center coordinates \((-2, 5)\), the equation becomes  
   \[
   (x + 2)^2 + (y - 5)^2 = r^2
   \]

3. **Find the Radius \(r\)**: Since the circle passes through (1, 9), substitute these values into the equation to solve for \(r^2\).  
   \[
   (1 + 2)^2 + (9 - 5)^2 = r^2 \\
   3^2 + 4^2 = r^2 \\
   9 + 16 = r^2 \\
   r^2 = 25
   \]

4. **Write the Complete Equation**: Substitute \(r^2 = 25\) back into the equation from step 2.  
   \[
   (x + 2)^2 + (y - 5)^2 = 25
   \]  

**Final Answer:**  
\boxed{(x+2)^2 + (y-5)^2 = 25}

First, recall the standard form of an ellipse's equation when it's centered at the origin. If the major axis is horizontal, the equation is \(\frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1\), where \(2a\) is the length of the major axis and \(2b\) is the length of the minor axis.

Given that the major axis is 10, which means \(2a = 10\). So, solving for \(a\), I get \(a = 5\). Then, squaring \(a\) gives me \(a^2 = 25\).

Similarly, the minor axis is given as 6, so \(2b = 6\). Solving for \(b\), I find \(b = 3\). Squaring this, I get \(b^2 = 9\).

Putting these values into the standard equation, it becomes \(\frac{x^2}{25} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1\).
</think>

The given problem involves finding the equation of an ellipse centered at (0, 0) with specific axes lengths.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the Standard Form:**
   - The standard form of an ellipse's equation when it is centered at the origin (0, 0) and has a horizontal major axis is:
     \[
     \frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
     \]
     where \(2a\) is the length of the major axis, and \(2b\) is the length of the minor axis.

2. **Determine \(a\) and \(b\):**
   - Given that the major axis (horizontal) has a length of 10:
     \[
     2a = 10 \Rightarrow a = 5
     \]
     Squaring \(a\) gives:
     \[
     a^2 = 25
     \]
     
   - Given that the minor axis (vertical) has a length of 6:
     \[
     2b = 6 \Rightarrow b = 3
     \]
     Squaring \(b\) gives:
     \[
     b^2 = 9
     \]

3. **Substitute into the Standard Form:**
   - Plugging the values of \(a^2\) and \(b^2\) into the standard equation:
     \[
     \frac{x^2}{25} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1
     \]

**Final Answer:**  
The equation of the ellipse is \(\boxed{\dfrac{x^2}{25} + \dfrac{y^2}{9} = 1}\).

To convert the given equation into the standard form of an ellipse, follow these steps:

**Given Equation:**
\[ 9x^2 + 4y^2 = 36 \]

**Step 1:** Divide both sides by 36 to normalize the equation.
\[
\frac{9x^2}{36} + \frac{4y^2}{36} = \frac{36}{36}
\]
Simplifying:
\[
\frac{x^2}{4} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1
\]

**Step 2:** Compare the simplified equation with the standard form of an ellipse.
The standard form of an ellipse centered at \((h, k)\) is:
\[
\frac{(x - h)^2}{a^2} + \frac{(y - k)^2}{b^2} = 1
\]
Since there are no shifts in \(x\) or \(y\), \(h = 0\) and \(k = 0\).

**Final Standard Form:**
\[
\boxed{\dfrac{x^2}{4} + \dfrac{y^2}{9} = 1}
\]

In this case, the foci are at (40, 0) and (-40, 0), which means they're on the x-axis because their y-coordinates are zero. So, the major axis must be along the x-axis.

The distance between each focus and the center of the ellipse is called 'c'. Since the foci are at (40, 0) and (-40, 0), the center of the ellipse should be exactly halfway between them. Calculating that midpoint: average of 40 and -40 is zero, so the center is at (0, 0). Therefore, each focus is 40 units away from the center, so c = 40.

Next, I know the length of the major axis is given as 100. The major axis is twice the semi-major axis 'a', so a must be half of that. So, a = 100 / 2 = 50.

Now, for an ellipse, there's this relationship between a, b (the semi-minor axis), and c: 

a² = b² + c²

I can rearrange this to solve for b:

b² = a² - c²

Plugging in the values I have:

a is 50, so a² is 2500.

c is 40, so c² is 1600.

So,

b² = 2500 - 1600 = 900

Taking the square root of both sides gives b = √900 = 30.

Now that I have a and b, I can write the standard equation of an ellipse centered at (h, k). Since our center is (0, 0), h = 0 and k = 0. Also, since the major axis is along the x-axis, the standard form is:

(x² / a²) + (y² / b²) = 1

Substituting the values I found:

x² / 50² + y² / 30² = 1

Which simplifies to:

x²/2500 + y²/900 = 1

So, that should be the equation of the ellipse.

The given problem involves finding the equation of an ellipse with foci at (40, 0) and (-40, 0), and a major axis length of 100. 

Firstly, since both foci lie on the x-axis, the major axis is along the x-axis, indicating that this is a horizontally oriented ellipse.

The distance between each focus and the center (which is at (0, 0)) is c = 40. The length of the major axis is given as 100, so the semi-major axis a is half of that, which is 50.

Using the relationship for an ellipse: 

a² = b² + c²

We can solve for b:

b² = a² - c²  
b² = 50² - 40²  
b² = 2500 - 1600  
b² = 900  
b = √900  
b = 30

Therefore, the standard equation of the ellipse is:

x²/2500 + y²/900 = 1

\(25 = 49 - b^2\)

The equation of the ellipse is determined to be:

$$\frac{x^2}{9} + \frac{y^2}{4} = 1$$

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify Given Points:** We have two points on the ellipse: (3, 0) and (0, 2).

2. **Assume Center at Origin:** Assuming the center of the ellipse is at the origin (0,0) simplifies the equation to:
   $$\frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1$$

3. **Plug in Points:**
   - For (3, 0):
     $$\frac{3^2}{a^2} + \frac{0^2}{b^2} = 1 \implies \frac{9}{a^2} = 1 \implies a^2 = 9 \implies a = 3$$
   - For (0, 2):
     $$\frac{0^2}{a^2} + \frac{2^2}{b^2} = 1 \implies \frac{4}{b^2} = 1 \implies b^2 = 4 \implies b = 2$$

4. **Equation of the Ellipse:** Substituting a and b back into the standard form:
   $$\frac{x^2}{9} + \frac{y^2}{4} = 1$$

5. **Verification:**
   - Check (3,0):
     $$\frac{9}{9} + 0 = 1 → True$$
   - Check (0,2):
     $$0 + \frac{4}{4} = 1 → True$$

Therefore, the equation of the ellipse is:

$$\boxed{\dfrac{x^2}{9} + \dfrac{y^2}{4} = 1}$$

   \]

   b^2 = 16 \Rightarrow b = 4
   \]

To determine the **विलक्षणता (eccentricity)** of the hyperbola given by the equation:

\[
\frac{y^2}{25} - \frac{x^2}{16} = 1
\]

follow these steps:

### Step 1: Identify the standard form of the hyperbola
The given equation is in the standard form of a **vertical hyperbola** (opens upwards and downwards):

\[
\frac{(y - k)^2}{a^2} - \frac{(x - h)^2}{b^2} = 1
\]

Here, \( h = 0 \) and \( k = 0 \), so the center of the hyperbola is at the origin.

### Step 2: Determine the values of \( a^2 \) and \( b^2 \)
From the equation:

- \( a^2 = 25 \) ⇒ \( a = 5 \)
- \( b^2 = 16 \) ⇒ \( b = 4 \)

### Step 3: Calculate the eccentricity (\( e \))
The formula for the eccentricity of a hyperbola is:

\[
e = \sqrt{1 + \frac{b^2}{a^2}}
\]

Substitute the values of \( a^2 \) and \( b^2 \):

\[
e = \sqrt{1 + \frac{16}{25}} = \sqrt{\frac{41}{25}} = \frac{\sqrt{41}}{5}
\]

### Final Answer
The eccentricity of the hyperbola is:

\[
\boxed{\text{Final Answer: } \frac{\sqrt{41}}{5}}
\]

\]

\[

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the Standard Form:**  
   The given equation is in the standard form of a hyperbola centered at the origin, opening horizontally:
   \[
   \frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
   \]
   
2. **Determine \( a \) and \( b \):**  
   Comparing with the standard form:
   - \( a^2 = 4 \) ⇒ \( a = 2 \)
   - \( b^2 = 9 \) ⇒ \( b = 3 \)

3. **Find the Foci:**  
   For hyperbolas, the distance from the center to each focus is given by:
   \[
   c^2 = a^2 + b^2
   \]
   Substituting the known values:
   \[
   c^2 = 4 + 9 = 13 ⇒ c = \sqrt{13}
   \]
   Since it's a horizontal hyperbola, the foci are located at \( (\pm\sqrt{13}, 0) \).

4. **Find the Vertices:**  
   The vertices lie along the transverse axis (x-axis) and are at a distance of \( a \) from the center:
   - Vertices: \( (-2, 0) \) and \( (2, 0) \)

5. **Determine the Asymptotes:**  
   For a hyperbola opening horizontally, the asymptote equations are:
   \[
   y = \pm\frac{b}{a}x
   \]
   Substituting \( b = 3 \) and \( a = 2 \):
   - Asymptotes: \( y = \frac{3}{2}x \) and \( y = -\frac{3}{2}x \)

**Final Answer:**  
- **Foci:** \( (\pm\sqrt{13}, 0) \)
- **Vertices:** \( (-2, 0) \) and \( (2, 0) \)
- **Asymptotes:** \( y = \frac{3}{2}x \) and \( y = -\frac{3}{2}x \)

To find the equation of the parabola, we start by noting that the directrix is a vertical line \(x = -1\), indicating a horizontal parabola (opening left or right). The standard form for such a parabola is:

\[ y^2 = 4p(x - h) \]

where \((h + p, k)\) is the focus and \(x = h - p\) is the directrix. Given the directrix \(x = -1\), we have:

\[ h - p = -1 \Rightarrow h = p - 1 \]

Substituting \(h\) into the parabola equation:

\[ y^2 = 4p(x - (p - 1)) \]
\[ y^2 = 4p(x - p + 1) \]

Using the point \((2, 3)\):

\[ 3^2 = 4p(2 - p + 1) \]
\[ 9 = 4p(3 - p) \]
\[ 4p^2 - 12p + 9 = 0 \]

Solving this quadratic equation:

\[ p = \frac{12 \pm \sqrt{(12)^2 - 4 \cdot 4 \cdot 9}}{2 \cdot 4} \]
\[ p = \frac{12}{8} = \frac{3}{2} \]

Thus, \(h = \frac{1}{2}\) and the equation becomes:

\[ y^2 = 6(x - \frac{1}{2}) \]
\[ y^2 = 6x - 3 \]

**Answer:**  
The equation of the parabola is \(\boxed{y^2 = 6x - 3}\).

To find the **vertex form** of the given quadratic equation:

\[ Y = 3x^2 + 6x + 5 \]

we can complete the square. Here's a step-by-step explanation:

### Step 1: Start with the given equation
\[ Y = 3x^2 + 6x + 5 \]

### Step 2: Factor out the coefficient of \( x^2 \) from the first two terms
Here, the coefficient is **3**.

\[ Y = 3(x^2 + 2x) + 5 \]

### Step 3: Complete the square inside the parentheses
To complete the square for \( x^2 + 2x \):
- Take half of the coefficient of \( x \), which is \( 1 \).
- Square it to get \( 1^2 = 1 \).

\[ Y = 3(x^2 + 2x + 1 - 1) + 5 \]
\[ Y = 3[(x + 1)^2 - 1] + 5 \]

### Step 4: Distribute the coefficient and simplify
Multiply **3** with both terms inside the brackets:

\[ Y = 3(x + 1)^2 - 3 + 5 \]
\[ Y = 3(x + 1)^2 + 2 \]

### Final Answer:
The vertex form of the given parabola is:
\[ \boxed{Y = 3(x + 1)^2 + 2} \]

To determine the center and radius of the circle defined by the equation:

\[
X^2 + y^2 + 6x - 4y - 12 = 0
\]

we'll rewrite it in the standard form of a circle's equation, which is:

\[
(x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
\]

where \((h, k)\) is the center and \(r\) is the radius.

**Step 1: Group Like Terms**

First, group the terms involving \(x\) and \(y\) separately:

\[
X^2 + 6x + y^2 - 4y = 12
\]

**Step 2: Complete the Square for \(x\) Terms**

To complete the square for the \(x\)-terms:

\[
X^2 + 6x \quad \text{becomes} \quad (X + 3)^2 - 9
\]

So,

\[
(X + 3)^2 - 9 + y^2 - 4y = 12
\]

**Step 3: Complete the Square for \(y\) Terms**

To complete the square for the \(y\)-terms:

\[
y^2 - 4y \quad \text{becomes} \quad (y - 2)^2 - 4
\]

So,

\[
(X + 3)^2 - 9 + (y - 2)^2 - 4 = 12
\]

**Step 4: Combine Constants**

Combine the constants on the left side:

\[
(X + 3)^2 + (y - 2)^2 - 13 = 12
\]

Move the constant to the right side:

\[
(X + 3)^2 + (y - 2)^2 = 25
\]

**Step 5: Identify the Center and Radius**

Now, compare with the standard form \((x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2\):

- **Center:** \((-h, -k) = (-3, 2)\)
- **Radius:** \(r = \sqrt{25} = 5\)

**Final Answer:**

The center of the circle is at \((-3, 2)\) and its radius is \(5\).

The given equation \(x^2 + y^2 - 4x + 2y - 11 = 0\) can be rewritten in the standard form of a circle by completing the square.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Group the x terms and y terms together:**
   \[
   (x^2 - 4x) + (y^2 + 2y) = 11
   \]

2. **Complete the square for the x terms:**
   - Take \(x^2 - 4x\).
   - The coefficient of \(x\) is -4. Half of this is -2, and squaring it gives 4.
   - Add and subtract 4:
     \[
     (x^2 - 4x + 4) - 4 = (x - 2)^2 - 4
     \]

3. **Complete the square for the y terms:**
   - Take \(y^2 + 2y\).
   - The coefficient of \(y\) is 2. Half of this is 1, and squaring it gives 1.
   - Add and subtract 1:
     \[
     (y^2 + 2y + 1) - 1 = (y + 1)^2 - 1
     \]

4. **Combine the completed squares and constants:**
   \[
   (x - 2)^2 - 4 + (y + 1)^2 - 1 = 11
   \]
   Simplify:
   \[
   (x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 - 5 = 11
   \]

5. **Move the constant term to the right side:**
   \[
   (x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 16
   \]

So, the equation in standard form is:
\[
(x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 4^2
\]
Which means the circle has a center at (2, -1) and a radius of 4.

**Final Answer:**
The standard form of the given equation is \((x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 16\), so the final answer is:
\[
\boxed{\text{Standard form of the circle: } (x-2)^2 + (y+1)^2 = 16}
\]

\[
x^2 + 3x - 4 = 0
\]

(x + 4)(x - 1) = 0
\]

\[

\[

\]

For \( x = 1 \):
\[

\]
The other point of intersection is \( (1, 4) \).

To determine the points where the line \( y = x + 3 \) intersects the circle \( x^2 + y^2 = 17 \), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Substitute the Line Equation into the Circle's Equation
Since \( y = x + 3 \), substitute this expression for \( y \) into the circle's equation:
\[
x^2 + (x + 3)^2 = 17
\]

### Step 2: Expand and Simplify
Expand the squared term:
\[
x^2 + x^2 + 6x + 9 = 17
\]
Combine like terms:
\[
2x^2 + 6x + 9 - 17 = 0 \quad \Rightarrow \quad 2x^2 + 6x - 8 = 0
\]

### Step 3: Simplify the Quadratic Equation
Divide the entire equation by 2 to simplify:
\[
x^2 + 3x - 4 = 0
\]

### Step 4: Factor the Quadratic Equation
Factor the quadratic equation:
\[
(x + 4)(x - 1) = 0
\]
This gives two solutions for \( x \):
\[
x = -4 \quad \text{or} \quad x = 1
\]

### Step 5: Find Corresponding \( y \)-Values
Substitute each \( x \)-value back into the line equation \( y = x + 3 \):

- For \( x = -4 \):
  \[
  y = (-4) + 3 = -1
  \]
  
- For \( x = 1 \):
  \[
  y = 1 + 3 = 4
  \]

### Final Answer: Points of Intersection
The points where the line intersects the circle are:
\[
\boxed{\{(-4, -1), (1, 4)\}}
\]

The given equation \( X^2 + y^2 - 8x - 6y + 20 = 0 \) can be rewritten in the standard form of a circle by completing the square.

1. Group x terms and y terms:
   \( (x^2 - 8x) + (y^2 - 6y) + 20 = 0 \)

2. Complete the square for each group:
   - For x: \( x^2 - 8x \) becomes \( (x - 4)^2 - 16 \)
   - For y: \( y^2 - 6y \) becomes \( (y - 3)^2 - 9 \)

3. Substitute back into the equation:
   \( (x - 4)^2 - 16 + (y - 3)^2 - 9 + 20 = 0 \)

4. Simplify constants:
   \( (x - 4)^2 + (y - 3)^2 + ( -16 - 9 + 20 ) = 0 \)
   \( (x - 4)^2 + (y - 3)^2 + (-5) = 0 \)

5. Move the constant to the other side:
   \( (x - 4)^2 + (y - 3)^2 = 5 \)

The equation is now in standard form, revealing the center at (4, 3) and radius √5.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the Center and Radius:**
   - The center of the circle is given as \((h, k) = (1, 2)\).
   
2. **Use a Point on the Circle to Find the Radius (\(r\)):**
   - Since the point \((4, 6)\) lies on the circumference of the circle, it must satisfy the equation of the circle.
   - Substitute \(x = 4\) and \(y = 6\) into the equation:
     \[
     (4 - 1)^2 + (6 - 2)^2 = r^2
     \]
     \[
     3^2 + 4^2 = r^2
     \]
     \[
     9 + 16 = r^2
     \]
     \[
     25 = r^2
     \]
   - Therefore, \(r^2 = 25\).

3. **Formulate the Complete Equation of the Circle:**
   - Substitute \(h = 1\), \(k = 2\), and \(r^2 = 25\) back into the standard equation:
     \[
     (x - 1)^2 + (y - 2)^2 = 25
     \]

**Final Answer:**

The equation of the circle is:

\[
(x - 1)^2 + (y - 2)^2 = 25
\]

The given hyperbola is in standard form: \( \frac{x^2}{4} - \frac{y^2}{9} = 1 \).

- **Center**: (0, 0)
- **Vertices**: Located at a distance of \( a = 2 \) from the center along the x-axis. So, vertices are at (-2, 0) and (2, 0).
- **Foci**: Calculated using \( c^2 = a^2 + b^2 \), where \( c = \sqrt{13} \). Thus, foci are at (-\( \sqrt{13} \), 0) and (\( \sqrt{13} \), 0).
- **Asymptotes**: Equations are \( y = \pm \frac{b}{a}x = \pm \frac{3}{2}x \).

\[
\frac{y^2}{a^2} - \frac{x^2}{b^2} = 1
\]

Here, 'a' is the distance from the center to each vertex along the y-axis, and 'b' is related to the conjugate axis.

The distance between the foci is given by 2c. In this case, since the foci are at (0, ±√10), the value of c is √10. So,

\[
c = \sqrt{a^2 + b^2}
\]

Wait, no, that's not right. For hyperbolas, the relationship between a, b, and c is:

\[
c^2 = a^2 + b^2
\]

So if c is √10, then:

\[

10 = a^2 + b^2
\]

\[
\frac{3^2}{a^2} - \frac{2^2}{b^2} = 1 \\
\frac{9}{a^2} - \frac{4}{b^2} = 1
\]

1. \( a^2 + b^2 = 10 \)
2. \( \frac{9}{a^2} - \frac{4}{b^2} = 1 \)

I need to solve these simultaneously to find a² and b².

Let me denote A = a² and B = b² for simplicity.

So,

1. \( A + B = 10 \)
2. \( \frac{9}{A} - \frac{4}{B} = 1 \)

From equation 1, I can express B as:

\[
B = 10 - A
\]

Substitute this into equation 2:

\[
\frac{9}{A} - \frac{4}{10 - A} = 1
\]

Now, let's solve for A.

Multiply both sides by \( A(10 - A) \) to eliminate denominators:

\[
9(10 - A) - 4A = A(10 - A)
\]

Expanding the terms:

\[
90 - 9A - 4A = 10A - A^2
\]
\[
90 - 13A = 10A - A^2
\]

Bring all terms to one side:

\[
A^2 - 23A + 90 = 0
\]

This is a quadratic equation in A. Let's solve it using the quadratic formula:

\[
A = \frac{23 \pm \sqrt{(23)^2 - 4(1)(90)}}{2}
\]
\[
A = \frac{23 \pm \sqrt{529 - 360}}{2}
\]
\[
A = \frac{23 \pm \sqrt{169}}{2}
\]
\[
A = \frac{23 \pm 13}{2}
\]

So, two possible solutions for A:

1. \( A = \frac{23 + 13}{2} = \frac{36}{2} = 18 \)
2. \( A = \frac{23 - 13}{2} = \frac{10}{2} = 5 \)

Now, check both solutions.

Case 1: If A = 18,

Then B = 10 - 18 = -8

But B can't be negative because it's b². So this case is invalid.

Case 2: If A = 5,

Then B = 10 - 5 = 5

So both a² and b² are 5? Wait, that would mean:

\[
c^2 = a^2 + b^2 \\
10 = 5 + 5
\]

Which checks out. So yes, A=5 and B=5.

Therefore,

\[
a^2 = 5 \\
b^2 = 5
\]

So plugging back into the standard form:

\[
\frac{y^2}{5} - \frac{x^2}{5} = 1
\]

Wait, but usually a hyperbola can't have a² and b² equal because then it's a rectangular hyperbola. Let me verify if this makes sense.

Yes, in fact, when a = b, the hyperbola is rectangular. So that's acceptable.

Thus, the equation is:

\[
\frac{y^2}{5} - \frac{x^2}{5} = 1
\]

The standard form of a horizontal hyperbola is \(\frac{(x - h)^2}{a^2} - \frac{(y - k)^2}{b^2} = 1\). Its asymptotes have slopes \(±\frac{b}{a}\). Given that one of the slopes is 2, it follows that \(\frac{b}{a} = 2\).

Without specific values for \(a\) and \(b\), the equation remains in terms of their relationship. Therefore, the hyperbola's asymptotes have a slope of ±2.

The slope of the asymptotes is \boxed{2}.

To determine the equation of the hyperbola given its vertices at (0,5) and (0,-3), we first calculate the center. The midpoint between these vertices gives us the center:

Center = ((0 + 0)/2, (5 + (-3))/2) = (0,1)

Next, since the vertices lie along the y-axis, this is a vertical hyperbola. For a vertical hyperbola centered at (h,k), the standard form is:
(y - k)^2 / a² - (x - h)^2 / b² = 1

The distance from the center to each vertex (a) is calculated as:
For the upper vertex (0,5): |5 - 1| = 4 → a = 4
Similarly for the lower vertex: |-3 - 1| = 4 → a = 4

Now, without additional information about foci or another point on the hyperbola, we cannot determine b² because it requires knowledge of c (the distance from center to each focus) via the relationship:
c² = a² + b²

Since there's insufficient data, the equation cannot be uniquely determined.

Alright, let's try to figure out this problem. So, we need to find the equation of a parabola with its vertex at (0, 0) and focus at (0, 4). Hmm, okay.

First off, I remember that the standard form of a parabola depends on whether it opens upwards, downwards, left, or right. Since the vertex is at (0, 0), which is the origin, things might be simpler here.

The general form for a parabola that opens upward or downward is \( y = ax^2 \). I think in this case, since the focus is above the vertex, it should open upwards. So, let me recall how to relate the focus to the equation.

I remember that for a parabola \( y = ax^2 \), the distance from the vertex to the focus (which is given as 4 units here) is related to 'a'. Specifically, I think the formula is \( p = \frac{1}{4a} \), where 'p' is the distance from the vertex to the focus. So, if p is 4, then we can solve for 'a'.

Let me write that down: 

\( p = \frac{1}{4a} \)

We know \( p = 4 \), so substituting:

\( 4 = \frac{1}{4a} \)

Now, solving for 'a':

Multiply both sides by 4a to get rid of the denominator:

\( 4 * 4a = 1 \)

Which simplifies to:

\( 16a = 1 \)

So,

\( a = \frac{1}{16} \)

Wait, does that make sense? If 'a' is positive and less than 1, then the parabola will open upwards but be wider than one with a larger coefficient. Yes, that seems right because as 'a' decreases (becomes smaller), the parabola becomes wider.

Now, plugging 'a' back into the standard equation:

\( y = ax^2 \)

First, I recognize that the given equation Y = 3x² + 6x + 5 represents a parabola. To find its vertex form, which is necessary to identify the vertex (h, k), I need to complete the square.

Starting with the standard form of a quadratic equation, Y = ax² + bx + c, here a = 3, b = 6, and c = 5.

I'll factor out the coefficient of x² from the first two terms: Y = 3(x² + 2x) + 5.

Next, to complete the square inside the parentheses, I take half of the coefficient of x, which is 1, square it to get 1, and add and subtract this value within the equation. This gives me:

Y = 3(x² + 2x + 1 - 1) + 5.

I then rewrite it as Y = 3[(x + 1)² - 1] + 5.

Distributing the 3 outside, I get Y = 3(x + 1)² - 3 + 5.

Finally, combining like terms results in Y = 3(x + 1)² + 2. This is now in vertex form, where h = -1 and k = 2. Therefore, the vertex of the parabola is at (-1, 2).

To find the vertex (shreerakar) form of the given quadratic equation \( Y = 3x^2 + 6x + 5 \), we'll follow these steps:

### Step 1: Start with the Given Equation
\[ Y = 3x^2 + 6x + 5 \]

### Step 2: Factor Out the Coefficient of \( x^2 \)
First, factor out the coefficient of \( x^2 \) (which is 3) from the first two terms:
\[ Y = 3(x^2 + 2x) + 5 \]

### Step 3: Complete the Square
To complete the square inside the parentheses:
1. Take half of the coefficient of \( x \), which is \( 2/2 = 1 \).
2. Square this value: \( (1)^2 = 1 \).

Add and subtract this squared value inside the equation:
\[ Y = 3\left(x^2 + 2x + 1 - 1\right) + 5 \]
\[ Y = 3\left((x + 1)^2 - 1\right) + 5 \]

### Step 4: Distribute and Simplify
Distribute the 3 outside the parentheses:
\[ Y = 3(x + 1)^2 - 3 + 5 \]
Combine like terms:
\[ Y = 3(x + 1)^2 + 2 \]

### Final Answer: Vertex Form of the Equation
The vertex form of the given equation is:
\[
Y = 3(x + 1)^2 + 2
\]

This shows that the vertex (shreerakar) of the parabola represented by the original equation is at \( (-1, 2) \).

For (-1,3):

The parabola passing through the points (2,8), (-1,3), and (0,4) can be determined using these steps. 

First, substitute each point into the general form of a quadratic equation \( y = ax^2 + bx + c \).

- Using (0,4): \( 4 = c \).
  
Substitute this back into the equation to get:
\[ y = ax^2 + bx + 4 \]

Next, plug in (2,8):
\[ 8 = a(2)^2 + b(2) + 4 \]
\[ 4a + 2b = 4 \]   → Equation 1

And (-1,3):
\[ 3 = a(-1)^2 + b(-1) + 4 \]
\[ a - b = -1 \]      → Equation 2

Solve Equations 1 and 2 simultaneously:
- Multiply Equation 2 by 2: \( 2a - 2b = -2 \)
- Add to Equation 1: 
  \[ 6a = 2 \]
  \[ a = \frac{1}{3} \]

Substitute \( a = \frac{1}{3} \) back into Equation 2:
\[ \frac{1}{3} - b = -1 \]
\[ b = \frac{4}{3} \]

Thus, the equation of the parabola is:
\[ y = \frac{1}{3}x^2 + \frac{4}{3}x + 4 \]

**Final Answer:**  
The equation of the parabola is \( y = \frac{1}{3}x^2 + \frac{4}{3}x + 4 \).

The given equation of the circle can be rewritten in standard form by completing the square for both \(X\) and \(Y\). 

1. Group terms:
   \[
   (X^2 + 6X) + (Y^2 - 4Y) = 12
   \]

2. Complete the square for each group:
   - For \(X^2 + 6X\), add and subtract \(9\):
     \[
     X^2 + 6X = (X + 3)^2 - 9
     \]
   - For \(Y^2 - 4Y\), add and subtract \(4\):
     \[
     Y^2 - 4Y = (Y - 2)^2 - 4
     \]

3. Substitute back into the equation:
   \[
   (X + 3)^2 - 9 + (Y - 2)^2 - 4 = 12
   \]
   
4. Combine constants:
   \[
   (X + 3)^2 + (Y - 2)^2 - 13 = 12 \\
   (X + 3)^2 + (Y - 2)^2 = 25
   \]

The equation is now in standard form, revealing the center and radius.

To convert the given general form of a circle into its standard form, we need to complete the square for both \( x \) and \( y \) terms.

**Given Equation:**
\[
x^2 + y^2 - 4x + 2y - 11 = 0
\]

**Step 1:** Rearrange the equation to group like terms:
\[
(x^2 - 4x) + (y^2 + 2y) = 11
\]

**Step 2:** Complete the square for \( x \)-terms and \( y \)-terms.

- For \( x \):
  - Coefficient of \( x \) is \( -4 \).
  - Take half of it: \( \frac{-4}{2} = -2 \).
  - Square it: \( (-2)^2 = 4 \).

- For \( y \):
  - Coefficient of \( y \) is \( 2 \).
  - Take half of it: \( \frac{2}{2} = 1 \).
  - Square it: \( (1)^2 = 1 \).

**Step 3:** Add these squares to both sides:
\[
(x^2 - 4x + 4) + (y^2 + 2y + 1) = 11 + 4 + 1
\]

**Simplifying:**
\[
(x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 16
\]

So, the standard form of the circle is:
\[
\boxed{(x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 16}
\]

Wait, does that make sense? Let me double-check. The distance from the center (-2, 5) to the point (1, 9) should be equal to the radius. Using the distance formula: 

Distance = √[(x2 - x1)^2 + (y2 - y1)^2] = √[(1 - (-2))^2 + (9 - 5)^2] = √[(3)^2 + (4)^2] = √[9 + 16] = √25 = 5. Yep, so the radius is indeed 5, and \(r^2\) is 25.

So putting it all together, the equation of the circle should be:

\((x + 2)^2 + (y - 5)^2 = 25\).

I think that's it. Let me just write it out clearly.
</think>

The equation of the circle with center at (-2, 5) and passing through the point (1, 9) is found by determining the radius squared using the distance formula.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the Center:** The given center is \((-2, 5)\), so \(h = -2\) and \(k = 5\).

2. **Use the General Equation of a Circle:**
   \[
   (x + 2)^2 + (y - 5)^2 = r^2
   \]

3. **Find the Radius (\(r\)):** Since the circle passes through (1, 9), substitute these coordinates into the equation:
   \[
   (1 + 2)^2 + (9 - 5)^2 = r^2 \\
   3^2 + 4^2 = r^2 \\
   9 + 16 = r^2 \\
   r^2 = 25
   \]

4. **Write the Complete Equation:**
   \[
   (x + 2)^2 + (y - 5)^2 = 25
   \]

**Final Answer:**  
The equation of the circle is \((x + 2)^2 + (y - 5)^2 = 25\).

To find the intersection points between the line \( y = x + 3 \) and the circle \( x^2 + y^2 = 17 \), I'll substitute the expression for \( y \) from the line equation into the circle's equation.

This substitution will eliminate \( y \) and leave an equation in terms of \( x \) only. Solving this quadratic equation will give me the possible \( x \)-coordinates of the intersection points.

To determine the points where the line \( y = x + 3 \) intersects the circle \( x^2 + y^2 = 17 \), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Substitute the Line Equation into the Circle's Equation
Start by substituting \( y \) from the line equation into the circle's equation.

\[
x^2 + (x + 3)^2 = 17
\]

### Step 2: Expand and Simplify
Expand the squared term:

\[
x^2 + x^2 + 6x + 9 = 17
\]

Combine like terms:

\[
2x^2 + 6x + 9 - 17 = 0 \\
2x^2 + 6x - 8 = 0
\]

### Step 3: Solve the Quadratic Equation
Divide the entire equation by 2 to simplify:

\[
x^2 + 3x - 4 = 0
\]

Factor the quadratic equation:

\[
(x + 4)(x - 1) = 0
\]

So, the solutions for \( x \) are:

\[
x = -4 \quad \text{and} \quad x = 1
\]

### Step 4: Find Corresponding \( y \)-Values
Substitute these \( x \)-values back into the line equation \( y = x + 3 \):

- For \( x = -4 \):
  
  \[
  y = (-4) + 3 = -1
  \]
  
  So, one point is \( (-4, -1) \).

- For \( x = 1 \):
  
  \[
  y = 1 + 3 = 4
  \]
  
  So, the other point is \( (1, 4) \).

### Final Answer

The intersection points are:

\[
\boxed{\{ -4 : -1, 1 : 4 \}}
\]

2. **Determine 'a':** The length of the major axis is 100, so semi-major axis \(a = \frac{100}{2} = 50\).

3. **Find 'c':** The distance from the center to each focus is half the distance between foci: \(c = 40\) (since foci are at ±40 on x-axis).

   \[
   40^2 = 50^2 - b^2 \\
   1600 = 2500 - b^2 \\
   b^2 = 900 \\
   b = 30
   \]

5. **Plug into Equation:** Substituting \(a^2 = 2500\) and \(b^2 = 900\), the equation becomes:
   \[
   \frac{x^2}{2500} + \frac{y^2}{900} = 1
   \]

The equation of the ellipse centered at (4, -3) with a vertical major axis length of 14 and a horizontal minor axis length of 10 is:

\[
\frac{(x - 4)^2}{25} + \frac{(y + 3)^2}{49} = 1
\]

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the Standard Form:**  
   For an ellipse centered at (h, k), with a vertical major axis, the standard equation is:
   \[
   \frac{(x - h)^2}{b^2} + \frac{(y - k)^2}{a^2} = 1
   \]
   where '2a' is the length of the major axis and '2b' is the length of the minor axis.

2. **Determine a and b:**  
   Given that the major axis (vertical) has a length of 14, so:
   \[
   2a = 14 \implies a = 7
   \]
   The minor axis (horizontal) has a length of 10, so:
   \[
   2b = 10 \implies b = 5
   \]

3. **Plug in the Values:**  
   Substitute h = 4, k = -3, a = 7, and b = 5 into the standard form:
   \[
   \frac{(x - 4)^2}{5^2} + \frac{(y - (-3))^2}{7^2} = 1
   \]

The given problem involves finding the equation of a hyperbola centered at the origin (0,0) with foci at (0, ±5) and vertices at (0, ±3). 

First, we identify that this is a vertical hyperbola because the foci and vertices are aligned vertically. The standard form for such a hyperbola is:

\[
\frac{y^2}{a^2} - \frac{x^2}{b^2} = 1
\]

Here:
- \( a = 3 \) (distance from center to each vertex)
- \( c = 5 \) (distance from center to each focus)

Using the relationship \( c^2 = a^2 + b^2 \), we solve for \( b^2 \):

\[
c^2 = a^2 + b^2 \\
5^2 = 3^2 + b^2 \\
25 = 9 + b^2 \\
b^2 = 16
\]

Substituting these values into the standard form equation gives:

\[
\frac{y^2}{9} - \frac{x^2}{16} = 1
\]

- **Vertices**: Located at (±a, 0), where \( a = 2 \). So, vertices are at \( (2, 0) \) and \( (-2, 0) \).
  
- **Foci**: Located at (±c, 0), where \( c^2 = a^2 + b^2 \). Here, \( c = \sqrt{4 + 9} = \sqrt{13} \). Thus, the foci are at \( (\sqrt{13}, 0) \) and \( (-\sqrt{13}, 0) \).
  
- **Asymptotes**: Given by \( y = \pm \frac{b}{a}x \), which simplifies to \( y = \pm \frac{3}{2}x \).
